String Diagrams for Optics
Guillaume Boisseau

Abstract
Optics are a data representation for compositional data access, with lenses as a popular special case. Hedges has presented a diagrammatic calculus for lenses, but in a way that does not generalize to other classes of optic. We present a calculus that works for all optics, not just lenses; this is done by embedding optics into their presheaf category, which naturally features string diagrams. We apply our calculus to the common case of lenses, extend it to effectful lenses, and explore how the laws of optics manifest in this setting.



University of Oxford, United Kingdomhttps://orcid.org/0000-0001-5244-893XWork funded by the EPSRC

1
Introduction

Optics
are a versatile categorical structure. Their best-known
special case,
lenses
, have found uses in a variety of contexts,
from machine learning to game theory

[
Hed18
]
. Their more general
instantiations have been studied in the context of bidirectional data
transformations
[
Ril18
]
. In all cases,
their main feature of interest is their composability and their peculiar
bidirectional information flow.

In the interest of making them easier to represent and manipulate,
authors often spontaneously use diagrams to construct instances of
optics
[
PGW17
,
Ril18
]
. These
diagrams are usually informal, with one notable exception in the work of
Hedges
[
Hed17
]
on diagrams for
lenses. Hedges’ diagrammatic calculus however assumes a lot of structure
on the underlying categories, in a way that doesn’t extend to more
general optics.

Here we propose instead a different approach that embeds optics into a
larger space (namely its presheaf category) that naturally has string
diagrams. Not only does this work for the most general optics, but all
the diagrammatic gadgets follow naturally from the embedding, and it
even allows for useful diagrams that would not be expressible in the
category
\mathit{Optic}
alone.

2
Background

We fix a monoidal category
(M,\otimes,I,\lambda,\mu,a)

throughout the paper.

We assume readers are familiar with
coends
. For an introduction
to the material relevant to the study of optics, see
[
Rom20
, Chapter
2]
.

{note*}

We will prefer diagrammatic order for composition, using the symbol

\fatsemi
.

2.1
Actegories

Definition 1

(
[
nLa
]
)
.

An
M
-actegory (contraction of “action” and “category”) is a
category
C
equipped with a functor
{\odot_{C}}:M\times C\rightarrow C

(the “action”) and two natural structure isomorphisms

\lambda_{x}:I\odot_{C}x\xrightarrow{\sim}x
and

a_{m,n,x}:(m\otimes n)\odot_{C}x\xrightarrow{\sim}m\odot_{C}(n\odot_{C}x)

that satisfy compatibility axioms with the monoidal structure of
M
.

We will drop the subscripts when the relevant actegory is clear from
context. The naming of the structure morphisms clashes with those of

M
on purpose:

Proposition 2
.

M
has canonically the structure of an
M
-actegory, with

\odot_{M}=\otimes
, and
\lambda
and
a
as the actegory
structure morphisms.

In what follows, when we use
M
as an
M
-actegory, we assume this
canonical structure.

2.2
Optics

Definition 3

(
[
Rom20
, Proposition 3.1.1]
)
.

Given two
M
-actegories
C
and
D
, we construct the category

\mathit{Optic}_{C,D}
as follows: objects are pairs
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
u\end{smallmatrix}\right)
where

x:C
and
u:D
, and arrows are elements of the set

\mathit{Optic}_{C,D}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
u\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y\\
v\end{smallmatrix}\right)):=\int^{m:M}C(x,m\odot_{C}y)\times D(m\odot_{D}v,u)

Given
\alpha:C(x,m\odot_{C}y)
and
\beta:D(m\odot_{D}v,u)
, we
will denote the corresponding arrow by
\langle\alpha\mathop{|}\beta\rangle_{m}
.
Composition and identities are defined componentwise in the expected
way; see
[
Rom20
]
for more
details.

{note*}

Expanding the definition of coends in
Set
, we get that the coend
above denotes the set of pairs
\langle\alpha\mathop{|}\beta\rangle_{m}
with

\alpha:C(x,m\odot_{C}y)
and
\beta:D(m\odot_{D}v,u)
, quotiented
by the equation

\langle\alpha\fatsemi(f\odot_{C}y)\mathop{|}\beta\rangle_{m}=\langle\alpha\mathop{|}(f\odot_{D}v)\fatsemi\beta\rangle_{n}

for
f:M(n,m)
.

Except in special cases, this category is not monoidal. This prevents us
from having string diagrams in the usual way. We will see how to work
around this limitation in the rest of the paper.

Example 4
.

The canonical example of optics are lenses. They
arise when
C=D=M
and the monoidal structure of
C
is
cartesian. We get:

\mathit{Lens}_{C}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
u\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y\\
v\end{smallmatrix}\right)):=\int^{c:C}C(x,c\times y)\times C(c\times v,u)

While this presentation is pleasantly symmetrical, lenses are usually
described as a pair of functions without this unfamiliar coend. We can
in fact calculate that both presentations are equivalent:

\displaystyle\mathit{Lens}_{C}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
u\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y\\
v\end{smallmatrix}\right))

\displaystyle=\int^{c:C}C(x,c\times y)\times C(c\times v,u)

\displaystyle\cong\int^{c:C}C(x,y)\times C(x,c)\times C(c\times v,u)

\displaystyle\cong C(x,y)\times\int^{c:C}C(x,c)\times C(c\times v,u)

\displaystyle\cong C(x,y)\times C(x\times v,u)

We recover the usual formulation: a lens from
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
u\end{smallmatrix}\right)
to

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y\\
v\end{smallmatrix}\right)
is a pair of functions
get:x\rightarrow y
and

put:x\times v\rightarrow u
. The intuition is that
get
extracts some

y
from a datum
x
, and
put
allows replacing that
y
by a
new
v
, yielding an updated datum
u
. It is often the case that

x=u
and
y=v
, making this intuition clearer, but having
distinct types allows for more flexibility.

A concrete example of a lens that gives access to a field of a record
can be written in Haskell:

data Lens x u y v = L (x -> y) (x -> v -> u)

data Person = P { name :: String, address :: String }
personName :: Lens Person Person String String
personName = L get put
where get (P name _) = name
put (P _ address) name = P name address

The case for distinct types is well illustrated on tuples:

tupleSnd :: Lens (a, b) (a, c) b c
tupleSnd = L get put
where get (_, b) = b
put (a, _) c = (a, c)

\lipicsEnd

2.3
Tambara Modules

Definition 5

(
[
Rom20
, Proposition 5.1.1]
)
.

Given two
M
-actegories
C
and
D
, we construct the category

\mathit{Tamb}_{C,D}
as follows: objects are (pro)functors

P:C^{op}\times D\rightarrow Set
equipped with a natural transformation

\mathit{strength}:\int_{m:M}P(a,b)\rightarrow P(m\odot_{C}a,m\odot_{D}b)

compatible with the actegory structures; arrows are

\mathit{strength}
-preserving natural transformations.

This generalizes the usual notion of strength for a profunctor.

Definition 6
.

We construct the bicategory
\mathit{Tamb}
as follows: objects are

M
-actegories; Hom-categories are the categories
\mathit{Tamb}_{C,D}
.

It inherits its bicategorical structure from the bicategory
\mathit{Prof}
of
profunctors: the identities are the hom-profunctors

C(\mathord{-},\mathord{=})
, and the tensor (horizontal composition)
is profunctor composition, defined as usual as follows:

(P\otimes Q)(a,c)=\int^{b}P(a,b)\times Q(b,c)

{note*}

\mathit{Prof}
and
\mathit{Tamb}
share in fact a lot of structure. In a sense

\mathit{Tamb}
is the analogue of
\mathit{Prof}
for
M
-actegories, and we will
see that like
\mathit{Prof}
it supports a rich diagrammatic calculus.

Our interest in Tambara modules comes from the following strong
relationship with optics:

Theorem 7

(
[
Rom20
, Proposition 5.5.2]
)
.

[\mathit{Optic}_{C,D}^{op},Set]\cong\mathit{Tamb}_{C,D}

Proof.

The proof can be found in
[
Rom20
, Proposition
5.5.2]
, but initially comes from

[
PS07
, Proposition 6.1]
in the
special case where
M=C=D
, along with more results on the
structure of both of those categories.

∎

3
Diagrams for Tambara
Modules

3.1
Basics

As in any bicategory, cells in
\mathit{Tamb}
can be represented as diagrams,
as follows:

A 0-cell (an
M
-actegory) is represented as a planar region delimited
by the other types of cells. For technical reasons we will not represent
them in what follows, but it should be kept in mind that 1-cells can
only be composed if their types match.

A 1-cell
P:\mathit{Tamb}_{C,D}
is represented as a wire, with
C
above
and
D
below:

P
P

Tensoring (1-cell composition) is vertical juxtaposition (for

P:\mathit{Tamb}_{C,D}
and
Q:\mathit{Tamb}_{D,E}
):

For example, one could represent the following complex composition of
cells diagrammatically:

P
\alpha{}
\alpha{}
\epsilon{}
\epsilon{}
S
R
Q

The axioms of bicategories ensure that we can interchange boxes like we
do in string diagrams for monoidal categories.

3.2
Oriented Wires

So far, this was common to any bicategory. We can now investigate
gadgets specific to
\mathit{Tamb}
.

Let us fix an
M
-actegory
C
.

Definition 8
.

Given
x:C
, let us define two profunctors

R_{x}:=C(\mathord{-},\mathord{=}\odot_{C}x)
and

L_{x}:=C(\mathord{-}\odot_{C}x,\mathord{=})
.

Proposition 9
.

R_{x}
is in
\mathit{Tamb}_{C,M}
and
L_{x}
is in
\mathit{Tamb}_{M,C}
, where

M
is taken with its canonical
M
-actegory structure.

Proof.

R_{x}
is a profunctor
C^{op}\times M\rightarrow Set
. The action of the
(
m\odot_{C}\mathord{-}
) functor provides it with a strength. The same
works for
L_{x}
.

∎

Proposition 10
.

R_{x}
extends to a functor
R:C\rightarrow\mathit{Tamb}_{C,M}
, and
L_{x}

extends to a functor
L:C^{op}\rightarrow\mathit{Tamb}_{M,C}

Proof.

Straightforward from their definitions.

∎

Proposition 11
.

R
and
L
respect the actegory
structures:
R_{I}\cong L_{I}\cong M(-,=)
,

R_{x}\otimes R_{m}\cong R_{m\odot x}
, and

L_{m}\otimes L_{x}\cong L_{m\odot x}
.

Proof.

See appendix
A.1
.

∎

This justifies the following notation:

(4)

{note*}

This choice of notation could create confusion as to whether a box on an
oriented wire is meant to be seen as in the image of
R
/
L
or not.
However we will see later that
R
and
L
are fully faithful, and
thus this confusion fades away: all boxes on an oriented wire are arrows
in
C
.

From the propositions above, we see that this notation respects
composition in
C
as well as the
M
-actegory structures (note the
inversion that happens when tensoring on a right-oriented wire):

{note*}

Note that because of the types of the 1-cells (that are not shown in the
diagrams), not all tensorings of the oriented wires are allowed. For
example, it could be tempting to think that

R_{x}\otimes R_{y}\cong R_{y\otimes x}
for
x,y:C
, but not only
is
C
not monoidal in general, the tensoring doesn’t even type-check
since both
R_{x}
and
R_{y}
are objects of
\mathit{Tamb}_{C,M}
.

{note*}

When
C
is chosen to be
M
, both
R
and
L
provide a
monoidal embedding of
M
into
\mathit{Tamb}_{M,M}
; we will see later that
it is also fully faithful. This means that the string diagrams in
M

have two full and faithful embeddings into the string diagrams of

\mathit{Tamb}
, using the oriented wires.

3.3
Bending Wires

Proposition 12
.

For a given
x:C
, the modules
R_{x}
and

L_{x}
are adjoint. Moreover, the structure maps of the adjunction are
dinatural in
x
.

Proof.

R_{x}=C(\mathord{-},\mathord{=}\odot x)
and

L_{x}=C(\mathord{-}\odot x,\mathord{=})
are clearly adjoint in

\mathit{Prof}
. The adjunction lifts to
\mathit{Tamb}
; see appendix

A.2
. Dinaturality in
x
is straightforward from
the definition of the unit and counit.

∎

This means that there exist two 2-cells, that we will draw as:

(8)

We have discovered an additional property of the diagrammatic language:
oriented arrows can be bent downwards. Note that bending upwards is not
in general possible.

{note*}

In the case of set-based lenses (i.e.
C=D=M=Set
with the cartesian
product), the second of those maps (the “cap”) was featured in the
calculus of
[
Hed17
]
. The first
map (the “cup”) however cannot be expressed in that calculus.

4
Embedding Optics

4.1
A Representation Theorem

We will now use this calculus to express optics. Recall from

Theorem 7
that presheaves on optics are equivalent to
Tambara modules. Consequently, the Yoneda embedding

Y:\mathit{Optic}_{C,D}\rightarrow[\mathit{Optic}_{C,D}^{op},Set]\cong\mathit{Tamb}_{C,D}

provides a fully faithful embedding of optics into
\mathit{Tamb}
. This is
the crucial property that enables our calculus.

Lemma 13
.

Y\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
u\end{smallmatrix}\right)=R_{x}\otimes L_{u}

Proof.

By definition of
Y
,
R
and
L
, modulo the equivalence of

Theorem 7
.

∎

Thus
Y\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
u\end{smallmatrix}\right)
has the following nice diagrammatic notation:

(9)

From this we deduce the main theorem of this paper:

Theorem 14

(Representation theorem)
.

Optics
l:\mathit{Optic}_{C,D}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
u\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y\\
v\end{smallmatrix}\right))
are in bijection
with arrows in
\mathit{Tamb}_{C,D}
of type:

u
x
l
l
v
y

and moreover this bijection is functorial, i.e. composition of optics
becomes horizontal composition of diagrams and the identity optic is the
identity diagram.

Proof.

By full-faithfulness and functoriality of the Yoneda embedding.

∎

The consequences of this property need stressing: any diagram of this
type represents an optic,
even if it is made of subcomponents that
are not themselves optics
. A parallel can be drawn with complex
numbers: a complex number with no imaginary part represents a real
number, regardless of whether it was constructed (using complex
operations like rotation) from complex numbers that were not themselves
real numbers. In both cases, we can work in this more general space
(complex numbers/Tambara modules) to reason more flexibly about the
simpler objects (reals/optics).

For example, the following diagram is a valid optic, even though several
of its subcomponents are not optics.

u
x
k
k
l
l
v
y

4.2
Simple Arrows

The simplest optic we can construct is made out of two simple arrows
(i.e. arrows in the base
M
-actegories). This is sometimes called an

adapter
. Given
f:C(x,y)
and
g:D(v,u)
, we can see from
its type that
R_{f}\otimes L_{g}
is an optic:

u
x
\vphantom{f}g
\vphantom{f}g
f
f
v
y

Lemma 15
.

The optic corresponding to this diagram is

\langle f\fatsemi\lambda^{-1}_{y}\mathop{|}\lambda_{v}\fatsemi g\rangle_{I}
.

Proof.

By a straightforward calculation; see appendix

A.3
.

∎

The special case of a single simple arrow is particularly interesting:

Theorem 16
.

All morphisms of type

R_{x}
l
l
R_{y}

are of the form

x
f
f
y

for some unique
f:C(x,y)
.

Similarly for
L
and wires going to the left.

Proof.

Since
L_{I}\cong M(-,=)
, we have (using a potentially confusing
notation):

\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\begin{gathered}\hbox to115.08pt{\vbox to23.03pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 57.53987pt\lower-11.51321pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

Thus by the representation theorem,
l
can be seen as an optic in

\mathit{Optic}_{C,M}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
I\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y\\
I\end{smallmatrix}\right))
. We then calculate (see
appendix
A.4
) that

\mathit{Optic}_{C,M}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
I\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y\\
I\end{smallmatrix}\right))\cong C(x,y)
, with the
reverse direction given by the action of
R
. The proof for
L
is
identical.

∎

Corollary 17
.

R
and
L
are fully faithful.

{note*}

As pointed out earlier, in the particular case where we choose
C=D=M

(as in the case of lenses), then
R
and
L
both provide a
fully-faithful
and monoidal
embedding of the arrows in
M
into
diagrams.

4.3
Refining the Representation
Theorem

Together, simple arrows and the cap are enough to represent any optic as
a string diagram.

Theorem 18
.

Given
\alpha:C(x,m\odot y)
and

\beta:D(m\odot v,u)
, the optic
l:=\langle\alpha\mathop{|}\beta\rangle_{m}

can be represented as follows:

(10)

Proof.

By calculating the composition of the pair of simple arrows with the
cap; see appendix
A.5
.

∎

{note*}

Recall that the pairs
\langle\alpha\mathop{|}\beta\rangle_{m}
are defined modulo an
equivalence relation. How is this compatible with the diagrammatic
notation? The equivalence says that

\langle\alpha\fatsemi(f\odot y)\mathop{|}\beta\rangle_{m}=\langle\alpha\mathop{|}(f\odot v)\fatsemi\beta\rangle_{n}
;
diagrammatically, this becomes:

(11)

Which we already know holds, by sliding
f
along the bent wire!

5
Applications

We present two examples of applications of the calculus that illustrate
its expressivity.

5.1
Lawful Optics

One of the most striking consequences of this calculus (and the question
that led to its discovery) is the neatness with which it can express
optic laws.

As originally constructed by the Haskell community

[
Kme20
]
, optics were required to abide by certain
round-trip laws that ensure coherence of their operations. Those laws in
particular coincide with very-well-behavedness

[
FGM+05
]
in the case of lenses,
which we investigate in more detail in the next section. Riley
formalized those laws in a general form
[
Ril18
, Section
3]
, but the result is rather hard to
manipulate. The string calculus enables an alternative (and equivalent)
description that is purely diagrammatic:

Definition 19
.

An optic
l:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
x\end{smallmatrix}\right)\rightarrow\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y\\
y\end{smallmatrix}\right)
is said to be lawful when

\begin{gathered}\hbox to106.06pt{\vbox to34.86pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 53.02988pt\lower-17.42873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-49.69687pt}{-8.5359pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{gathered}

(13)

{note*}

We can see that lawful optics are exactly the homomorphisms for the
“pair-of-pants” comonoid made from pairs of oriented wires.
Interestingly, if we view this comonoid as a procomonad on
C
, then
lawful optics are in bijection with its coalgebras on the carrier

R_{x}
. This is a significant generalization of the result by O’Connor

[
O’C10
]
that lawful lenses are the
coalgebras for the store comonad: here the “pair-of-pants” procomonad
precisely generalizes the store comonad.

Theorem 20
.

This notion of lawfulness is equivalent to the one
defined by Riley in
[
Ril18
, Section 3]
.

Proof.

See appendix
A.6
.

∎

Thus this diagrammatic definition captures properly the useful and very
general notion of lawfulness for optics. Using this theorem, many
properties of lawfulness can be derived purely diagrammatically. As an
example, let us reprove
[
Ril18
, Proposition
3.0.4]
:

Proposition 21

(
[
Ril18
, Proposition 3.0.4]
)
.

If
\alpha
and
\beta
are mutual inverses, then the optic

\langle\alpha\mathop{|}\beta\rangle_{m}
is lawful.

Proof.

5.2
Cartesian Lenses

The canonical special case of optics, that we mentioned in

4
, is cartesian lenses. They arise when we
restrict ourselves to
C=D=M
and the monoidal product of
C
is
cartesian.

In this setting, we have two important gadgets in
C
: duplication and
deletion, corresponding respectively to the diagonal map

C(x,x\times x)
and the terminal map
C(x,I)
. Diagrammatically,
we represent them as follows:

.

Given
f:C(X,Y\times Z)
, we have

\begin{gathered}\hbox to105.58pt{\vbox to35.83pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 52.7886pt\lower-17.91484pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

(14)

Proof.

This corresponds to the standard fact that

f=\langle fst\circ f,snd\circ f\rangle
.

∎

Theorem 23
.

A lens
l:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
u\end{smallmatrix}\right)\rightarrow\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y\\
v\end{smallmatrix}\right)
can be expressed as:

u
x
put
put
get
get
v
y

for some
get:C(x,y)
and
put:C(x\times v,u)
.

Proof.

∎

{note*}

Observe that it is diagrammatically clear that the definition of
put

and
get
in terms of
\langle\alpha\mathop{|}\beta\rangle_{m}
respects the
equivalence relation induced by the coend.

We recovered purely diagrammatically the usual formulation of lenses in
terms of
get
and
put
, that we had derived in

4
. In this setting, various properties of lenses
can be investigated purely diagrammatically. As an example, let us
revisit
[
Ril18
, Proposition 3.0.3]
, which
captures the fact that the general notion of lawfulness for optics
coincides with the familiar PutGet, GetPut and PutPut laws

[
FGM+05
]
(together called
“very-well-behavedness”) in the case of lenses.

Proposition 24

(
[
Ril18
, Proposition 3.0.3]
)
.

A lens
l:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
x\end{smallmatrix}\right)\rightarrow\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y\\
y\end{smallmatrix}\right)
is lawful iff the following
three laws (respectively called PutGet, GetPut and PutPut) hold in C:

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{gathered}\end{array}

It is straightforward to see that the PutPut and the GetPut laws
together entail this equality. By applying the deletion map successively
to the outputs, one can also show that this equation entails those two
laws, when
y
is inhabited.

∎

5.3
Effectful Lenses

We now turn to a less common example: effectful lenses. They stem from
the desire to allow lenses to perform effects while retrieving or
updating data. Various approaches have been proposed; see Abou-Saleh et
al.
[
ACG+16
]
for an overview.

Let
C
be a cartesian category and
T
a monad on
C
. We would
like an optic that resembles cartesian lenses from the previous section,
but with effectful arrows. This means that we would like our arrows to
live in the Kleisli category
C_{T}
. This category however is rarely
monoidal, let alone cartesian: for it to be monoidal, the monad
T

would need to be commutative, which rules out large classes of effects
that we might want to use. Thus we cannot reuse the results from the
previous section. Here we can instead make good use of the generality of
monoidal actions:
C_{T}
may not be monoidal, but when
T
is strong
(which is rather common), the product of
C
extends to an action of

C
on
C_{T}

[
Ril18
, Proposition
4.9.3]
. This is enough to define an optic for
monadic lenses:

\mathit{MLens}_{T}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
u\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y\\
v\end{smallmatrix}\right)):=\int^{c:C}C_{T}(x,c\times y)\times C_{T}(c\times v,u)

Let us now investigate the diagrams for such an optic. Recall the
details of how oriented wires are typed. Here the acting category is

C
, which means that in a diagram like the following, the typing
rules enforce that
x
,
y
and
f
can live in
C_{T}
, but

a
,
b
and
g
can only live in
C
.

a
x
\vphantom{f}g
\vphantom{f}g
f
f
b
y

This is why we don’t need
C_{T}
to be monoidal: this calculus only
allows an arrow in
C_{T}
to be tensored with arrows in
C
. This
gives us a string diagram calculus where otherwise none would have been
possible.

The distinction between effectful maps (in
C_{T}
) and pure maps (in

C
) is an important aspect of this calculus. Note that every pure map

f
can be lifted to an effectful map written
{}^{\scriptscriptstyle\bm{\lceil}}\mkern-1.0muf{}^{\scriptscriptstyle\bm{\rceil}}
, via a
canonical functor. This functor also respects the actegory structures,
and therefore allows us to embed the pure lenses from the previous
section as monadic lenses.

This calculus even inherits some of the diagrammatic features of the
previous section: the duplication map and the swap still exist and are
represented as before.

(15)

The difference is that the bottom wire can only carry maps living in

C
. Whereas before, all maps could be “slid through” the
duplication map and swap, now only
C
-maps (aka pure maps) can:

(16)

We now restrict ourselves to the monadic lenses proposed by Abou-Saleh
et al.
[
ACG+16
]
. Those lenses
are modeled closer to ordinary lenses, in particular their definition
does not involve a coend. They are composed of
get:C(x,y)
and

put:C_{T}(x\times v,u)
. Diagrammatically they look quite like
cartesian lenses:

u
x
put
put
{}^{\scriptscriptstyle\bm{\lceil}}\mkern-1.0muget{}^{\scriptscriptstyle\bm{\rceil}}
{}^{\scriptscriptstyle\bm{\lceil}}\mkern-1.0muget{}^{\scriptscriptstyle\bm{\rceil}}
v
y

Note that
get
is required to be pure. This is important to ensure
that composing two such lenses stays of that simplified shape. This
non-trivial fact can be seen diagrammatically in what follows: if

get
was not pure, it couldn’t be slid across the duplication map.

6
Conclusion and Future Work

We have presented a calculus that flowed naturally from the Yoneda
embedding of optics into Tambara modules. We have shown that it was
well-suited for expressing common properties of optics and proving
useful theorems generally, some of which would otherwise be painful to
prove. This work however is only the start: it provides the basis of a
calculus, whose expressive power hasn’t yet been explored in the
plethora of topics where optics have found a use. In particular, we
expect new specific diagrammatic properties like those of lenses to
arise for other kinds of optics like prisms or traversals.

Then, the calculus could be linked with related constructions, like the
calculus for teleological categories from

[
Hed17
]
, or the Int construction
from
[
JSV96
]
.

Properties of
\mathit{Tamb}
as a bicategory also seem worth exploring, in
particular its strong similarity with
\mathit{Prof}
, and the link between
the properties of
M
and those of
\mathit{Tamb}
.

Finally, diagrams in
\mathit{Tamb}
with multiple ingoing and outgoing legs
seem to relate to combs as in

[
KU17
]
and dialogues in the
style of
[
Hed19
]
; there is potential for
using
\mathit{Tamb}
to provide a basis for general diagrammatic descriptions
of those objects.

Appendix A
Proofs

A.1
R
Respects the Actegory Structure
(
11
)

Proof (
11
).

\displaystyle R_{I}

\displaystyle=M(\mathord{-},\mathord{=}\odot_{M}I)

\displaystyle=M(\mathord{-},\mathord{=}\otimes I)

\displaystyle\cong M(\mathord{-},\mathord{=})

\displaystyle R_{x}\otimes R_{m}

\displaystyle=\int^{n:M}C(\mathord{-},n\odot_{C}x)\times M(n,\mathord{=}\odot_{M}m)

\displaystyle=\int^{n:M}C(\mathord{-},n\odot_{C}x)\times M(n,\mathord{=}\otimes m)

\displaystyle\cong C(\mathord{-},(\mathord{=}\otimes m)\odot_{C}x)

\displaystyle\cong C(\mathord{-},\mathord{=}\odot_{C}(m\odot_{C}x))

\displaystyle=R_{m\odot_{C}x}

\displaystyle L_{I}

\displaystyle=M(\mathord{-}\odot_{M}I,\mathord{=})

\displaystyle=M(\mathord{-}\otimes I,\mathord{=})

\displaystyle\cong M(\mathord{-},\mathord{=})

\displaystyle L_{m}\otimes L_{x}

\displaystyle=\int^{n:M}M(\mathord{-}\odot_{M}m,n)\times C(n\odot_{C}x,\mathord{=})

\displaystyle=\int^{n:M}M(\mathord{-}\otimes m,n)\times C(n\odot_{C}x,\mathord{=})

\displaystyle\cong C((\mathord{-}\otimes m)\odot_{C}x,\mathord{=})

\displaystyle\cong C(\mathord{-}\odot_{C}(m\odot_{C}x),\mathord{=})

\displaystyle=L_{m\odot_{C}x}

It is easy to check that the corresponding strengths coincide as well.

∎

A.2
R
and
L
Are Adjoint
(
12
)

Proof (
12
).

The counit

\varepsilon:R_{x}\otimes L_{x}\rightarrow C(\mathord{-},\mathord{=})
of the
adjunction in
Prof
is given by composition in
C
. We need it
to commute with strength:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\int^{b}C(a,b\odot x)\otimes C(b\odot x,c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C(a,c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\int^{b^{\prime}}C(m\odot a,b^{\prime}\odot x)\otimes C(b^{\prime}\odot x,m\odot c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C(m\odot a,m\odot c)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fatsemi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{strength}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{strength}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fatsemi}

We inline the definition of
\mathit{strength}
, and move the coends out by
continuity, to get an equivalent square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C(a,b\odot x)\otimes C(b\odot x,c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C(a,c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C(m\odot a,m\odot(b\odot x))\otimes C(m\odot(b\odot x),m\odot c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C(m\odot a,m\odot c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C(m\odot a,(m\otimes b)\odot x)\otimes C((m\otimes b)\odot x,m\odot c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C(m\odot a,m\odot c)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fatsemi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(m\odot\mathord{-})\otimes(m\odot\mathord{-})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(m\odot\mathord{-})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fatsemi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C(id,a^{-1})\otimes C(a,id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fatsemi}

The top square commutes by functoriality of
(m\odot\mathord{-})
;
the bottom one by the fact that
a^{-1}\fatsemi a=id
.

Similarly, the unit also lives in
\mathit{Tamb}
. This is enough for the
adjunction to lift from
Prof
to
\mathit{Tamb}
.

∎

A.3
Diagram for Simple Arrows
(
15
)

Proof (
15
).

The diagram corresponds to the 2-cell
R_{f}\otimes L_{g}
.

It has type

\displaystyle R_{f}\otimes L_{g}

\displaystyle:R_{x}\otimes L_{u}\rightarrow R_{y}\otimes L_{v}

\displaystyle=\int_{ab}(\int^{m}R_{x}(a,m)\times L_{u}(m,b))\rightarrow(\int^{m}R_{y}(a,m)\times L_{v}(m,b))

And value

\displaystyle(R_{f}\otimes L_{g})(\langle p\mathop{|}q\rangle_{m})

\displaystyle=\langle R_{f}(p)\mathop{|}L_{g}(q)\rangle_{m}

\displaystyle=\langle p\fatsemi(m\odot f)\mathop{|}(m\odot g)\fatsemi q\rangle_{m}

To get the preimage through
Y
, we apply this map to the identity
optic.

\displaystyle(R_{f}\otimes L_{g})(id_{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
u\end{smallmatrix}\right)})

\displaystyle=(R_{f}\otimes L_{g})(\langle\lambda^{-1}_{x}\mathop{|}\lambda_{u}\rangle_{I})

\displaystyle=\langle\lambda^{-1}_{x}\fatsemi(I\odot f)\mathop{|}(I\odot g)\fatsemi\lambda_{u}\rangle_{I}

\displaystyle=\langle f\fatsemi\lambda^{-1}_{y}\mathop{|}\lambda_{v}\fatsemi g\rangle_{I}

∎

A.4
Simple Arrows Embed Fully-Faithfully
(
Theorem 16
)

Proof (
Theorem 16
).

We calculate:

\displaystyle\mathit{Optic}_{C,M}(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
I\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y\\
I\end{smallmatrix}\right))

\displaystyle=\int^{m}C(x,m\odot_{C}y)\times M(m\odot_{M}I,I)

\displaystyle=\int^{m}C(x,m\odot_{C}y)\times M(m\otimes I,I)

\displaystyle\cong\int^{m}C(x,m\odot_{C}y)\times M(m,I)

\displaystyle\cong C(x,I\odot_{C}y)

\displaystyle\cong C(x,y)

By following the isomorphisms, we get that the reverse direction is the
function

f:C(x,y)\mapsto\langle f\fatsemi\lambda_{y}^{-1}\mathop{|}\lambda_{I}\rangle_{I}
,
which as we saw previously corresponds to

f\mapsto\iota(f,id_{I})=R_{f}\otimes L_{id_{I}}=R_{f}
.

∎

A.5
Representation Theorem
(
Theorem 18
)

Lemma 25
.

The optic corresponding to this diagram is

\langle id_{m\odot x}\mathop{|}id_{m\odot u}\rangle_{m}
.

u
m
m
x
u
x

Proof.

Let us name the map corresponding to this diagram
\Lambda_{x,m,u}
.

Knowing the action of the cap
\varepsilon
, we obtain by a tedious
calculation that we will omit here:

\displaystyle\Lambda_{x,m,u}

\displaystyle:Y\left(\begin{smallmatrix}m\odot x\\
m\odot u\end{smallmatrix}\right)\rightarrow Y\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
u\end{smallmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\langle\alpha\mathop{|}\beta\rangle_{n}\mapsto\langle\alpha\fatsemi a_{n,m,x}^{-1}\mathop{|}a_{n,m,u}\fatsemi\beta\rangle_{n\otimes m}

Thus the corresponding optic is:

\displaystyle\Lambda_{x,m,u}(id_{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}m\odot x\\
m\odot u\end{smallmatrix}\right)})

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{x,m,u}(\langle\lambda^{-1}_{m\odot x}\mathop{|}\lambda_{m\odot u}\rangle_{I})

\displaystyle=\langle\lambda^{-1}_{m\odot x}\fatsemi a_{I,m,x}^{-1}\mathop{|}a_{I,m,u}\fatsemi\lambda_{m\odot u}\rangle_{I\otimes m}

\displaystyle=\langle\lambda^{-1}_{m}\odot x\mathop{|}\lambda_{m}\odot u\rangle_{I\otimes m}

\displaystyle=\langle(\lambda^{-1}_{m}\fatsemi\lambda_{m})\odot x\mathop{|}id_{m\odot u}\rangle_{m}

\displaystyle=\langle id_{m\odot x}\mathop{|}id_{m\odot u}\rangle_{m}

∎

Proof (
Theorem 18
).

The right-hand-side diagram is the composition of two optics of which we
know the value: the first is

\langle\alpha\fatsemi\lambda^{-1}_{m\odot y}\mathop{|}\lambda_{m\odot v}\fatsemi\beta\rangle_{I}
;
the second is
\langle id_{m\odot y}\mathop{|}id_{m\odot v}\rangle_{m}
.

The resulting optic is thus their composition:

\displaystyle\langle\alpha\fatsemi\lambda^{-1}_{m\odot y}\mathop{|}\lambda_{m\odot v}\fatsemi\beta\rangle_{I}\fatsemi\langle id_{m\odot y}\mathop{|}id_{m\odot v}\rangle_{m}

\displaystyle=\langle\alpha\fatsemi\lambda^{-1}_{m\odot y}\fatsemi(I\odot id_{m\odot y})\fatsemi a^{-1}_{m,I}\mathop{|}a_{m,I}\fatsemi(I\odot id_{m\odot v})\fatsemi\lambda_{m\odot v}\fatsemi\beta\rangle_{I\otimes m}

\displaystyle=\langle\alpha\fatsemi\lambda^{-1}_{m\odot y}\fatsemi a^{-1}_{m,I}\mathop{|}a_{m,I}\fatsemi\lambda_{m\odot v}\fatsemi\beta\rangle_{I\otimes m}

\displaystyle=\langle\alpha\fatsemi(\lambda^{-1}_{I}\odot y)\mathop{|}(\lambda_{I}\odot v)\fatsemi\beta\rangle_{I\otimes m}

\displaystyle=\langle\alpha\fatsemi(\lambda^{-1}_{I}\odot y)\fatsemi(\lambda_{I}\odot y)\mathop{|}\beta\rangle_{m}

\displaystyle=\langle\alpha\mathop{|}\beta\rangle_{m}

∎

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{gathered}\end{array}

The interesting insight is that the complicated
Optic^{2}_{M}
coend from
Riley’s paper can be easily constructed diagrammatically by tensoring
oriented wires as above. The theorem then follows directly from Riley’s
definition of lawfulness.

∎
